# Paper Audit and Review Report

This paper is **not ready** for submission to a strong conference in its current form. It has a genuinely sharp attack-surface framing and unusually good scope discipline for this topic, but the evidentiary base is too narrow, the realism gap is still a major rejection risk, and several novelty/statistics claims are not yet hardened against skeptical review. ## 1. Executive Verdict

**Overall grade:** 6.7/10

**Recommendation:** Weak reject

**One-paragraph verdict:** The paper’s scientific contract is clear: it studies a *post-training, score-level calibration-buffer contamination attack* against threshold-based federated IoT anomaly detection, with a single compromised client, fixed clean training/model/test pipeline, and three threshold policies on the nine-device N-BaIoT federation using autoencoders and FedAvg. It explicitly keeps training data, model weights, aggregation, test scores, and test labels unchanged, and it repeatedly limits claims to a score-level characterization rather than an end-to-end traffic-realizable attack. That is good and reviewer-safe. The problem is that the paper then asks the reader to treat this as a meaningful security result while validating it on only one dataset, one detector family, one FL algorithm, one threshold quantile, and a non-deployed score-injection proxy. A strong reviewer will say: interesting vulnerability surface, but not yet enough evidence for a high-confidence publication. ## 3. Top Strengths

1. **Strong scope discipline.** The paper repeatedly states that the attack changes only the calibration buffer and not model weights, gradients, training data, test scores, or test labels, which is exactly the right boundary for this topic. This appears in the abstract, threat model, isolation sanity check, discussion, and scope statements. ## 4. Top Weaknesses

1. **Near-fatal — realism gap:** The attack is only implemented as score replacement in the calibration buffer, not by generating traffic that actually induces those scores, and the paper admits traffic-level realizability is future work. Location: Abstract, §3 Threat Model, §6 Discussion and Limitations. 2. **Major — external validity is too narrow:** All claims rest on one dataset, nine devices, one detector family, one FL algorithm, one quantile, and one single-client compromise model. Location: §4.1 Dataset and Federation, §6 Scope limitations. 6. **Major — lowering attack interpretation is easy to misread:** The paper calls lowering “success” partly via positive \(\Delta\)TPR, but the real harm is increased false alarms and disparity, which is conceptually different from missed detections. ## 5. Fatal or Near-Fatal Risks

- **Score-level proxy may be judged insufficient as a security paper.** The paper itself says this is not a deployed attack and that end-to-end traffic realization is future work, which gives a reviewer a clean “interesting but incomplete” rejection basis. - **What would neutralize the objection:** Better figure design and explicit metric definitions.

## Final Verdict

**Current status:** not submission-ready.

**Expected reviewer confidence:** medium to high, because the paper is easy to understand, but that will make the realism gap and scope limitations even more visible.

**Probability of acceptance in current form:** about 25–35% for a solid conference, higher only if the venue strongly values tightly scoped initial characterizations over broader empirical validation.

**What score it could reach after fixes:** 8.5/10 without new experiments if wording, statistics, related work, and reproducibility are substantially improved; 9+/10 only if the paper adds broader empirical support or a stronger realism bridge.

**Exact top-priority fixes to reach 9/10+:**
1. Add a closest-work novelty table and tighten all universal language.  
2. Rewrite the statistics section into something simpler and more standard, or defend the gate framework rigorously.  
3. Reframe lowering as an alarm-burden attack, not a symmetric “success” story.  
4. Surface the scope limits in the abstract and conclusion more aggressively.  
5. Add appendix/artifact details.  
6. Expand beyond the single N-BaIoT / autoencoder / FedAvg setting before aiming high.

The paper has a real core worth preserving, but in its current form it is an *initial vulnerability note*, not yet a reviewer-proof conference paper.
